<ROLE>
Your primary role is to assist users by executing commands, modifying code, and solving technical problems effectively. You should be thorough, methodical, and prioritize quality over speed.
* If the user asks a question, like "why is X happening", don't try to fix the problem. Just give an answer to the question.
</ROLE>

<EFFICIENCY>
* Each action you take is somewhat expensive. Wherever possible, combine multiple actions into a single action, e.g. combine multiple bash commands into one, using sed and grep to edit/view multiple files at once.
* When exploring the codebase, use efficient tools like find, grep, and git commands with appropriate filters to minimize unnecessary operations.
</EFFICIENCY>

<FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>
* When a user provides a file path, do NOT assume it's relative to the current working directory. First explore the file system to locate the file before working on it.
* If asked to edit a file, edit the file directly, rather than creating a new file with a different filename.
* For global search-and-replace operations, consider using `sed` instead of opening file editors multiple times.
* NEVER create multiple versions of the same file with different suffixes (e.g., file_test.py, file_fix.py, file_simple.py). Instead:
  - Always modify the original file directly when making changes
  - If you need to create a temporary file for testing, delete it once you've confirmed your solution works
  - If you decide a file you created is no longer useful, delete it instead of creating a new version
* Do NOT include documentation files explaining your changes in version control unless the user explicitly requests it
* When reproducing bugs or implementing fixes, use a single file rather than creating multiple files with different versions
</FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>

<CODE_QUALITY>
* Write clean, efficient code with minimal comments. Avoid redundancy in comments: Do not repeat information that can be easily inferred from the code itself.
* When implementing solutions, focus on making the minimal changes needed to solve the problem.
* Before implementing any changes, first thoroughly understand the codebase through exploration.
* If you are adding a lot of code to a function or file, consider splitting the function or file into smaller pieces when appropriate.
* Place all imports at the top of the file unless explicitly requested otherwise or if placing imports at the top would cause issues (e.g., circular imports, conditional imports, or imports that need to be delayed for specific reasons).
</CODE_QUALITY>

<VERSION_CONTROL>
* If there are existing git user credentials already configured, use them and add Co-authored-by: openhands <openhands@all-hands.dev> to any commits messages you make. if a git config doesn't exist use "openhands" as the user.name and "openhands@all-hands.dev" as the user.email by default, unless explicitly instructed otherwise.
* Exercise caution with git operations. Do NOT make potentially dangerous changes (e.g., pushing to main, deleting repositories) unless explicitly asked to do so.
* When committing changes, use `git status` to see all modified files, and stage all files necessary for the commit. Use `git commit -a` whenever possible.
* Do NOT commit files that typically shouldn't go into version control (e.g., node_modules/, .env files, build directories, cache files, large binaries) unless explicitly instructed by the user.
* If unsure about committing certain files, check for the presence of .gitignore files or ask the user for clarification.
</VERSION_CONTROL>

<PULL_REQUESTS>
* **Important**: Do not push to the remote branch and/or start a pull request unless explicitly asked to do so.

</PULL_REQUESTS>

<PROBLEM_SOLVING_WORKFLOW>
1. EXPLORATION: Thoroughly explore relevant files and understand the context before proposing solutions

3. TESTING:

<ENVIRONMENT_SETUP>
* When user asks you to run an application, don't stop if the application is not installed. Instead, please install the application and run the command again.
* If you encounter missing dependencies:
  1. First, look around in the repository for existing dependency files (requirements.txt, pyproject.toml, package.json, Gemfile, etc.)

  3. Only install individual packages directly if no dependency files are found or if only specific packages are needed
* Similarly, if you encounter missing dependencies for essential tools requested by the user, install them when possible.
</ENVIRONMENT_SETUP>

<TROUBLESHOOTING>

  2. Assess the likelihood of each possible cause
  3. Methodically address the most likely causes, starting with the highest probability

<DOCUMENTATION>

  - Include explanations in your conversation responses rather than creating separate documentation files
  - If you need to create documentation files for reference, do NOT include them in version control unless explicitly requested
  - Never create multiple versions of documentation files with different suffixes
* If the user asks for documentation:
  - Confirm whether they want it as a separate file or just in the conversation
  - Ask if they want documentation files to be included in version control
</DOCUMENTATION>

<PROCESS_MANAGEMENT>
* When terminating processes:
  - Do NOT use general keywords with commands like `pkill -f server` or `pkill -f python` as this might accidentally kill other important servers or processes
  - Always use specific keywords that uniquely identify the target process
  - Prefer using `ps aux` to find the exact process ID (PID) first, then kill that specific PID
  - When possible, use more targeted approaches like finding the PID from a pidfile or using application-specific shutdown commands
</PROCESS_MANAGEMENT>

  4. Update tasks to "done" status immediately after completing each item

    19	[![Contributors](https://img.shields.io/github/contributors/jackdewinter/pymarkdown.svg)](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/graphs/contributors)
    20	[![Forks](https://img.shields.io/github/forks/jackdewinter/pymarkdown.svg)](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/network/members)

    23	
    24	PyMarkdown is primarily a Markdown Linter.  To ensure that the Markdown
    25	[linting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lint_%28software%29)
    26	is accomplished successfully, the rules engine that powers the linter
    27	uses a Markdown parser that is both
    28	[GitHub Flavored Markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/)
    29	compliant and
    30	[CommonMark](https://spec.commonmark.org/)
    31	compliant.  The rules provided in the base application can be easily extended
    32	by writing new plugins and importing them into the rules engine through simple
    33	configuration options.
    34	
    35	The PyMarkdown project has the following advantages:
    36	
    37	- Consistency
    38	  - This project can examine multiple files and directories with one invocation,
    39	    ensuring that all detected Markdown files adhere to the same guidelines.
    40	- Portable
    41	  - The linter runs on any system running Python 3.8 or later, with no modifications.
    42	- Standardized
    43	  - The parser that powers the linter is GitHub Flavored Markdown (GFM) compliant.
    44	    Due to that foundation, the parser does not guess how some parsers may handle
    45	    a given situation, as it has a clear set of rules to follow.
    46	- Accurate
    47	  - The parser passes all GFM conformance tests and CommonMark conformance tests.  In
    48	    test scenarios that were not present in either set of tests, the CommonMark
    49	    0.29.2 release was used to determine the correct parsing.
    50	- Flexible
    51	  - Each Markdown document is parsed into an internal token format.  Most of the rules
    52	    and made more efficient by leveraging this token format. Where that is not possible,
    53	    simple regular expressions and simple algorithms are used on a line-by-line basis.
    54	- Thoroughly tested
    55	  - The project currently has over 3200 scenario tests and coverage percentages
    56	    over 99%.
    57	- Extensible
    58	  - The parser for the project adheres to the GFM specification and most of
    59	    the rules for the parser leverage the tokens produced by that parser. The
    60	    [rules](/docs/rules.md) themselves are plugins, so they are extensible by default.
    61	    The parser itself will be extended as needed to provide for other Markdown
    62	    [extensions](/docs/extensions.md) as needed.
    63	- Versatile
    64	  - The PyMarkdown linter can be executed from a script on the command line,
    65	    from within another Python program, or using the popular Git Pre-Commit hooks.
    66	
    67	## Note
    68	
    69	This project is currently in beta, and some of these documented things
    70	may not work 100% as advertised until after the final release.  However,
    71	everything should be close enough to done that if you find something missing,
    72	please let us know.
    73	
    74	## GitHub Pre Commit Hooks
    75	
    76	If you intend to use the PyMarkdown project as a linter for your
    77	GitHub Pre-Commit hooks, then refer to [this document](/docs/pre-commit.md)
    78	on how to set that up.  Once setup, you can continue reading at the
    79	[How To Use section](#how-to-use) for more information on the options
    80	available for use in your Pre-Commit Hooks.
    81	
    82	## Requirements
    83	
    84	This project required Python 3.8 or later to function.
    85	
    86	## Installation
    87	
    88	```text
    89	pip install pymarkdownlnt
    90	```
    91	
    92	## How To Use
    93	
    94	### If You Get Stuck
    95	
    96	Full help support is available by entering
    97	
    98	```shell
    99	pymarkdown --help
   100	```
   101	
   102	on the command line and pressing enter.  For an individual command,
   103	help is available by following the command or commands with `--help`
   104	as follows:
   105	
   106	```shell
   107	pymarkdown scan --help
   108	```
   109	
   110	### Prerequisites
   111	
   112	Various sections of this document benefit from having concrete examples
   113	to illustrate how things work. For the following sections,
   114	this documentation will assume that there is a file called `example-1.md`
   115	in a directory called `examples` that has the following content:
   116	
   117	```Markdown
   118	## This is an example
   119	
   120	Just an example.
   121	```
   122	
   123	and a file called `example-2.md` in that same directory that has the
   124	following content:
   125	
   126	```Markdown
   127	# This is an example
   128	
   129	Just an example.
   130	
   131	```
   132	
   133	If you prefer concrete files, these files are checked into the
   134	[examples directory](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/tree/main/examples) of the GitHub project.
   135	
   136	### Basic Scanning
   137	
   138	The PyMarkdown linter is executed by calling the project from the
   139	command line and
   140	specifying one or more files and directories to scan for Markdown `.md`
   141	files.  The set of files and/or directories must be prefaced with the
   142	`scan` keyword to denote that scanning is required. For the examples
   143	directory, both this form:
   144	
   145	```shell
   146	pymarkdown scan examples
   147	```
   148	
   149	and this form:
   150	
   151	```shell
   152	pymarkdown scan examples/example-1.md examples/example-2.md
   153	```
   154	
   155	can be used to scan both files in the directory.  The only difference
   156	between the two invocations is that the first example will scan every
   157	Markdown `.md` file in the `examples` directory, while the second
   158	invocation will only scan the two specified files.  For clarity purposes,
   159	if the command line specifies the same file multiple times, that file
   160	name will only be added to the list of files to scan once.
   161	
   162	If everything is working properly, both of the above scans will
   163	produce the following output:
   164	
   165	```text
   166	examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character. (single-trailing-newline)
   167	```
   168	
   169	### Rules
   170	
   171	The PyMarkdown project includes 13 out-of-the-box [rules](/docs/rules.md),
   172	with another 29 rules to be added before the
   173	1.0.0 release.  These rules are implemented using a simple plugin
   174	system that is documented in the [developer documentation](/docs/developer.md).
   175	It is these rules that allow the PyMarkdown project to scan
   176	the various Markdown files, looking for bad patterns over that set of
   177	Markdown documents.
   178	
   179	Because of the way that the rules are provided, sometimes we
   180	refer to the rules as `rules` and sometimes as `rule plugins`.  A `rule`
   181	is a specific set of conditions that trigger the reporting of a violation
   182	when those conditions occur.  A `rule plugin` is the Python class
   183	and Python file in which the `rule` is supplied to the PyMarkdown application.
   184	Our goal is to try to not use these phrases interchangeably, but we are only
   185	human.  If we do mess up and use the wrong phase, we do apologize.
   186	
   187	Note that the initial set of rules are modelled after the 42 rules provided by
   188	David Anson's [Markdown Lint](https://github.com/markdownlint/markdownlint)
   189	project.  This decision was made
   190	to give Markdown authors that use his project in their IDEs (such as
   191	the MarkdownLint plugin for VSCode that I use), a good grounding
   192	in what they can consistently check for.
   193	
   194	### Rule Violation Format
   195	
   196	Executing either of the above example command lines will produce the following output:
   197	
   198	```text
   199	/examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character. (single-trailing-newline)
   200	```
   201	
   202	The format of the output for any rules that are triggered is as follows:
   203	
   204	`file-name:line:column: rule-id: description (aliases)`
   205	
   206	- `file-name` - Path to the file that triggered the rule.
   207	- `line`/`column` - Position in the file where the rule was triggered.
   208	- `rule-id` - Unique identifier assigned to the rule.
   209	- `description` - Human readable description of the rule.
   210	- `aliases` - One or more aliases used to reference the rule.
   211	
   212	For the rule violation that was reported at the start of this section,
   213	the first step in diagnosing
   214	that violation is to look at the file `/examples/example-1.md` at the end of
   215	line 3, which is column 16.  Rule [md047](/docs/rules/rule_md047.md) specifies
   216	that every file should end with a single newline character, which is
   217	what is reported in the violation's description.  Additionally, it reports that this
   218	rule can also be identified by the more human readable alias of
   219	`single-trailing-newline`.
   220	
   221	### Basic Configuration
   222	
   223	The most frequently used part of the configuration system is the
   224	part that enables and disables specific rules while scanning the
   225	Markdown files.  For example, if you do not like rule md047 which
   226	states that each file must end with a single newline, you can
   227	disable that rule by specifying:
   228	
   229	```shell
   230	pymarkdown -d md047 scan /examples
   231	```
   232	
   233	or:
   234	
   235	```shell
   236	pymarkdown --disable-rules md047 scan /examples
   237	```
   238	
   239	The effect of disabling the rule should be evidenced by
   240	the scan no longer reporting any violations of rule md047
   241	against the Markdown file `example-1.md`.
   242	
   243	Alternatively, rules can also be enabled.  As the modelled
   244	base rules for this project are based off those rules for David
   245	Anson's project, rule md002 is disable by default in both
   246	projects.  Specifically, rule md002 is disabled by default
   247	as rule md041 provides a better implementation of that rule
   248	that takes front-matter into account.  Until that rule is
   249	implemented, you can enable rule md002 by specifying either:
   250	
   251	```shell
   252	pymarkdown -e md002 scan /examples
   253	```
   254	
   255	or
   256	
   257	```shell
   258	pymarkdown --enable-rules md002 scan /examples
   259	```
   260	
   261	The effect of enabling the rule is evidenced by
   262	the scan reporting a violation of Rule md002 against
   263	Markdown file `example-1.md`:
   264	
   265	```text
   266	examples/example-1.md:1:1: MD002: First heading of the document should be a top level heading.
   267	 [Expected: h1; Actual: h2] (first-heading-h1, first-header-h1)
   268	examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character.
   269	(single-trailing-newline)
   270	```
   271	
   272	### Extensions
   273	
   274	Extensions are any features that are implemented in addition to the base
   275	[GitHub Flavored Markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/) specification.
   276	These extensions are documented in the [extensions](/docs/extensions.md)
   277	document, including information about how they perform, details on the
   278	extension, and configuration information.
   279

   301	## Open Issues and Future Plans
   302	
   303	During the development phase of this project, it was more useful to have
   304	an actual list of issues to track and prioritize, rather than relying on
   305	GitHub to do all the work. This is the location of the prioritized
   306	[Issues List](/issues.md).
   307	
   308	If you find any issues, please report them using the standard GitHub
   309	issues process.  When our team looks at your issue and triages
   310	it, it will be added to our Issues List with the triaged priority.
   311	For us, this provides transparency as to what we are currently working
   312	on, what is up next, and what our plans are for further development.

   326	the documentation, please file an issue [using GitHub](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/issues).
   327

   329	important, please file an issue
   330	[using GitHub](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/issues)
   331	that includes what you want to add, why you want to add it,
   332	and why it is important.  Please note that the issue will usually be
   333	the start of a conversation, and be ready for more questions.
   334	
   335	If you would like to contribute to the project in a more
   336	substantial manner, please contact me at `jack.de.winter` at `outlook.com`.

   135	if ERRORLEVEL 1 (

   138		goto error_end
   139	)
   140	
   141	if defined TEST_EXECUTION_FAILED (

   144

   149	echo {Publishing summaries from last test runs.}
   150	pipenv run %PTEST_PYSCAN_SCRIPT_PATH% --publish
   151	if ERRORLEVEL 1 (
   152		echo.
   153		echo {Publishing summaries from last test runs failed.}
   154		goto error_end
   155	)
   156	echo {Publishing summaries from last test runs succeeded.}
   157	
   158	:success_end
   159	rem Exit main part of script.
   160	echo.
   161	set PC_EXIT_CODE=0
   162	goto real_end
   163	
   164	:error_end
   165	set PC_EXIT_CODE=1
   166	if not defined PTEST_PUBLISH_SUMMARIES (
   167		echo {Execution of full test suite failed.}
   168	)
   169	
   170	:real_end
   171	erase /f /q %PTEST_TEMPFILE% > nul 2>&1

from test.utils import act_and_assert

1. The first block quote gets `   >    >    >` as its leading sequence
2. Then there are two newlines being added: `\n` and `\n`

Let me examine the actual issue more carefully by creating a simpler reproduction script:

## Phase 4. TEST CREATION: Create Reproduction Script<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3

"""
Reproduction script for the extra line issue in nested block quotes.
"""

import sys
import os
sys.path.insert(0, '.')

from pymarkdown.tokenized_markdown import TokenizedMarkdown
from pymarkdown.transform_to_gfm import TransformToGfm

def test_issue_reproduction():
    """Test the specific issue mentioned in the bug report."""
    
    # Test case from the issue
    source_markdown = """   >    >    >
   >    >    > list"""
    
    print("Source markdown:")
    print(repr(source_markdown))
    print()
    
    # Tokenize
    tokenizer = TokenizedMarkdown()
    tokens = tokenizer.transform(source_markdown)
    
    print("Tokens:")
    for i, token in enumerate(tokens):
        print(f"{i:2}: {token}")
    print()
    
    # Transform to HTML (should work correctly)
    transformer = TransformToGfm()
    html_output = transformer.transform(tokens)
    
    print("HTML output:")
    print(repr(html_output))
    print()
    
    # Now let's manually try to regenerate the markdown to see where the issue occurs
    # We'll use the test's transform_to_markdown for debugging
    
    from test.transform_to_markdown import TransformToMarkdown
    
    markdown_transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
    regenerated_markdown = markdown_transformer.transform(tokens)
    
    print("Regenerated markdown:")
    print(repr(regenerated_markdown))
    print()
    
    print("Expected:")
    expected = "   >    >    >\n   >    >    > list"
    print(repr(expected))
    print()
    
    print("Match:", regenerated_markdown == expected)
    
    if regenerated_markdown != expected:
        print("ISSUE REPRODUCED!")
        print("Difference:")
        print(f"Expected: {repr(expected)}")
        print(f"Actual:   {repr(regenerated_markdown)}")
        return False
    else:
        print("Issue not reproduced - test passes")
        return True

if __name__ == "__main__":

"""
Reproduction script for the extra line issue in nested block quotes.
"""

import sys
import os
import logging
sys.path.insert(0, '.')

from pymarkdown.tokenized_markdown import TokenizedMarkdown
from pymarkdown.transform_to_gfm import TransformToGfm
from pymarkdown.parser_logger import ParserLogger
from application_properties import ApplicationProperties
from pymarkdown.extension_manager import ExtensionManager

def test_issue_reproduction():
    """Test the specific issue mentioned in the bug report."""
    
    # Test case from the issue
    source_markdown = """   >    >    >
   >    >    > list"""
    
    print("Source markdown:")
    print(repr(source_markdown))
    print()
    
    # Proper initialization like in the test utils
    logging.getLogger().setLevel(logging.WARNING)
    ParserLogger.sync_on_next_call()

    tokenizer = TokenizedMarkdown()
    test_properties = ApplicationProperties()
    extension_manager = ExtensionManager()
    extension_manager.initialize(None, test_properties)
    extension_manager.apply_configuration()
    tokenizer.apply_configuration(test_properties, extension_manager)
    
    # Tokenize
    tokens = tokenizer.transform(source_markdown)
    
    print("Tokens:")
    for i, token in enumerate(tokens):
        print(f"{i:2}: {token}")
    print()
    
    # Transform to HTML (should work correctly)
    transformer = TransformToGfm()
    html_output = transformer.transform(tokens)
    
    print("HTML output:")
    print(repr(html_output))
    print()
    
    # Now let's manually try to regenerate the markdown to see where the issue occurs
    # We'll use the test's transform_to_markdown for debugging
    
    from test.transform_to_markdown import TransformToMarkdown
    
    markdown_transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
    regenerated_markdown = markdown_transformer.transform(tokens)
    
    print("Regenerated markdown:")
    print(repr(regenerated_markdown))
    print()
    
    print("Expected:")
    expected = "   >    >    >\n   >    >    > list"
    print(repr(expected))
    print()
    
    print("Match:", regenerated_markdown == expected)
    
    if regenerated_markdown != expected:
        print("ISSUE REPRODUCED!")
        print("Difference:")
        print(f"Expected: {repr(expected)}")
        print(f"Actual:   {repr(regenerated_markdown)}")
        return False
    else:
        print("Issue not reproduced - test passes")
        return True

if __name__ == "__main__":

1. The markdown source is: `   >    >    >\n   >    >    > list`
2. This gets parsed into tokens correctly
3. During regeneration, the process goes:

- The second newline comes from somewhere else

So there are three block quotes on the stack. The paragraph should generate the appropriate continuation for the second line.

But the paragraph processing doesn't do this - it only looks at its own `extracted_whitespace`.

This is the root cause of the bug!

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Clear Problem Statement

**Problem:** When regenerating markdown for nested block quotes spanning multiple lines, the paragraph token processing doesn't generate the appropriate block quote prefixes for continuation lines.

**Location:** In `test/transform_to_markdown.py`, the `__rehydrate_paragraph` method doesn't consider the container context (block quotes) when generating leading whitespace.

**How the test reproduces the issue:** 
1. Source: `   >    >    >\n   >    >    > list`
2. Gets parsed correctly into tokens
3. During regeneration:
   - First block quote line outputs: `   >    >    >`  
   - Blank tokens output: `\n\n` (two blanks at same position)
   - Paragraph token outputs: `` (empty, should be `   >    >    > `)
   - Text token outputs: `list`
4. Result: `   >    >    >\n\nlist` instead of `   >    >    >\n   >    >    > list`

**Best practices for the fix:**
- Minimal change to fix the specific issue
- Don't break existing functionality  
- Consider container context when generating paragraph leading whitespace
- Handle nested block quotes properly

**How to fix:** Modify the `__rehydrate_paragraph` method to check the container context and generate appropriate leading sequences for nested block quotes when the paragraph spans multiple lines.

## Phase 6. FIX IMPLEMENTATION

From the tokens:
5: [para(2,16):]

The extracted_whitespace is empty, but it should contain the block quote prefixes for the second line.

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Excellent! The simpler case works correctly. Notice the key difference in the debug trace:

In the working case:
```
1(2)>>container_line>>6-10:>:list:<
...
 -->container_line>list
 -->container_line>> > > list
```

The container_line gets properly prefixed with `> > > ` to become `> > > list`.

But in our failing case:
```
2(3)>>container_line>>16-20:>:list:<
...
 -->container_line>
 -->container_line>   >    >    > list
```

The container_line starts as empty (`>container_line>`) instead of `list`, and then gets the prefix.

This tells me the issue is that in the failing case, the paragraph processing returns an empty string instead of returning the text content that should then be processed by the container reconstruction.

Looking at the debug trace for our failing case:
```
post-h>new_data>
---
>>rehydrate_text>>list
<<rehydrate_text>>list
...
post-h>new_data>list
```

So the paragraph token processing returns an empty string, and the text token processing returns `list`. But in the working case, this is handled differently by the container reconstruction logic.

The difference is in how the container reconstruction processes the split text. In the working case, the split_container_text is `['> > >', 'list', '']`, while in the failing case it's `['   >    >    >', '', 'list', '']`.

The issue is that in the failing case, there's an empty string in the middle where there should be the properly prefixed text.

Let me look more carefully at what generates the container_text that gets split. From the debug:

Working case:
```
>>incoming>>:> > >\nlist\n:<<
```

Failing case:  
```
>>incoming>>:   >    >    >\n\nlist\n:<<
```

So the issue is upstream - the `container_text` that gets passed to `__apply_container_transformation` already has the wrong content.

The container_text comes from `transformed_data[record_item[1]:]` in the `__transform_container_end` method.

So `transformed_data` at the point of container end contains `   >    >    >\n\nlist\n` instead of `   >    >    >\nlist\n`.

This means that during the token processing, we accumulated:
1. `   >    >    >` (from block quote)
2. `\n` (from first blank)  
3. `\n` (from second blank)
4. `` (from paragraph) 
5. `list` (from text)

But it should be:
1. `   >    >    >` (from block quote)
2. `\n` (from blank)
3. `   >    >    > ` (from paragraph - this is missing!)  
4. `list` (from text)

So the core issue is that the paragraph token processing should return `   >    >    > ` instead of an empty string.

Looking at the `__rehydrate_paragraph` method again:

```python
def __rehydrate_paragraph(self, current_token, previous_token):
    # ...
    return ParserHelper.resolve_all_from_text(extracted_whitespace)
```

The `extracted_whitespace` for our paragraph token `[para(2,16):]` is empty, so it returns an empty string.

But it should return the block quote prefix for the current line context.

The fix is to modify `__rehydrate_paragraph` to check if we're in a block quote context and generate the appropriate leading sequence.

Let me examine how other similar methods handle this. Let me look at `__rehydrate_list_start`:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>view</arg_value><arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test/transform_to_markdown.py</arg_value><arg_key>view_range</arg_key><arg_value>[1090, 1120]</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test/transform_to_markdown.py:

</tool_response><|assistant|></think>I can see that `__rehydrate_list_start` calls `self.__look_for_last_block_token()` to find containing block quotes and uses that information.

The fix for `__rehydrate_paragraph` should be similar - it should check for containing block quotes and generate the appropriate leading sequence.

Let me implement the fix:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>str_replace</arg_value><arg_key>new_str</arg_key><arg_value>    def __rehydrate_paragraph(self, current_token, previous_token):
        """
        Rehydrate the paragraph block from the token.
        """
        _ = previous_token

        self.block_stack.append(current_token)
        current_token.rehydrate_index = 0
        extracted_whitespace = current_token.extracted_whitespace
        
        # Check if we're in a block quote context and need to generate leading spaces
        containing_block_quote_token = self.__look_for_last_block_token()
        if containing_block_quote_token and containing_block_quote_token.leading_spaces:
            # Get the appropriate leading space for this line
            split_leading_spaces = containing_block_quote_token.leading_spaces.split(
                ParserHelper.newline_character
            )
            if containing_block_quote_token.leading_text_index < len(split_leading_spaces):
                block_quote_prefix = split_leading_spaces[containing_block_quote_token.leading_text_index]
                # Only use the block quote prefix if we don't already have whitespace
                if not extracted_whitespace.strip():
                    extracted_whitespace = block_quote_prefix + " "

        if ParserHelper.newline_character in extracted_whitespace:
            line_end_index = extracted_whitespace.index(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            extracted_whitespace = extracted_whitespace[:line_end_index]
        return ParserHelper.resolve_all_from_text(extracted_whitespace)</arg_value><arg_key>old_str</arg_key><arg_value>    def __rehydrate_paragraph(self, current_token, previous_token):
        """
        Rehydrate the paragraph block from the token.
        """

    # ...

Each blank token ALWAYS returns a trailing newline. So two blank tokens = two newlines.

[Python interpreter: /opt/conda/envs/testbed/bin/python]
[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! Now I can see the key difference:

**Working case:**
```
1(2)>>container_line>>6-10:>:list:<
 -->container_line>list
 -->container_line>> > > list
<<transformed<<['> > >', '> > > list', '']
```

The container_line starts as `'list'` and gets transformed to `'> > > list'`.

**My failing case:**
```
1(2)>>container_line>>15-15:>::<
 -->container_line>
 -->container_line>   >    >    >
2(3)>>container_line>>16-20:>:list:<
 -->container_line>list
```

The container_line starts as empty `''` and gets transformed to `'   >    >    >'`, then the NEXT iteration processes `'list'` separately.

So in the working case, there are 3 container_lines total:
0. `'> > >'`  
1. `'list'` → becomes `'> > > list'`
2. `''`

In my case, there are 4 container_lines total:
0. `'   >    >    >'`
1. `''` → becomes `'   >    >    > '`  
2. `'list'`
3. `''`

The issue is that in my case, the empty line `''` is processed as a separate container_line, while in the working case, the `'list'` content is processed directly.

This difference comes from the split_container_text:
- Working: `['> > >', 'list', '']`  
- Mine: `['   >    >    >', '', 'list', '']`

So the fix should be to modify the container processing to handle the case where we have an empty element that should be merged with the following element.

Looking at the container processing logic, the issue is that when we encounter an empty `container_line`, we still apply the leading space transformation to it, turning `''` into `'   >    >    > '`. But then the following `'list'` is processed separately.

The fix should be to detect when we have an empty container_line that gets transformed into just leading spaces, and in such cases, merge it with the next element.

But this is getting quite complex. Let me think of a simpler approach.

Actually, let me look at what creates the difference in split_container_text in the first place.

Working case:
```
>>incoming>>:> > >\nlist\n:<<
>>split_container_text>>['> > >', 'list', '']
```

My case:
```
>>incoming>>:   >    >    >\n\nlist\n:<<  
>>split_container_text>>['   >    >    >', '', 'list', '']
```

The difference is that my incoming has `\n\n` (double newline) while the working case has `\n` (single newline).

This double newline comes from the two BLANK tokens both contributing `\n`.

So the cleanest fix might be to prevent the second BLANK token from contributing a newline when it immediately follows another BLANK token at the same position.

But since tokens are processed independently, let me try a different approach.

What if I modify the blank line processing to check if we've just processed another blank line?

import sys
sys.path.insert(0, '.')
from pymarkdown.tokenized_markdown import TokenizedMarkdown
from pymarkdown.transform_to_gfm import TransformToGfm
from pymarkdown.parser_logger import ParserLogger
from application_properties import ApplicationProperties
from pymarkdown.extension_manager import ExtensionManager
from test.transform_to_markdown import TransformToMarkdown
import logging

# Simple case (should still work)
source_markdown = '''> > >
> > > list'''

logging.getLogger().setLevel(logging.WARNING)
ParserLogger.sync_on_next_call()

tokenizer = TokenizedMarkdown()
test_properties = ApplicationProperties()
extension_manager = ExtensionManager()
extension_manager.initialize(None, test_properties)
extension_manager.apply_configuration()
tokenizer.apply_configuration(test_properties, extension_manager)

tokens = tokenizer.transform(source_markdown)
markdown_transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
regenerated = markdown_transformer.transform(tokens)

print('Simple case:')
print('Original:  ', repr(source_markdown))  
print('Regenerated:', repr(regenerated))
print('Match:', regenerated == source_markdown)
if regenerated == source_markdown:
    print('✅ Simple case works!')
else:
    print('❌ Simple case broken!')

import sys
sys.path.insert(0, '.')
from pymarkdown.tokenized_markdown import TokenizedMarkdown
from pymarkdown.transform_to_gfm import TransformToGfm
from pymarkdown.parser_logger import ParserLogger
from application_properties import ApplicationProperties
from pymarkdown.extension_manager import ExtensionManager
from test.transform_to_markdown import TransformToMarkdown
import logging

test_cases = [
    # Original issue case
    '   >    >    >\n   >    >    > list',
    
    # Simple case  
    '> > >\n> > > list',
    
    # Multiple paragraphs
    '> > para 1\n> >\n> > para 2',
    
    # Mixed content
    '> quote\n> \n> more quote',
]

logging.getLogger().setLevel(logging.WARNING)
ParserLogger.sync_on_next_call()

tokenizer = TokenizedMarkdown()
test_properties = ApplicationProperties()
extension_manager = ExtensionManager()
extension_manager.initialize(None, test_properties)
extension_manager.apply_configuration()
tokenizer.apply_configuration(test_properties, extension_manager)

markdown_transformer = TransformToMarkdown()

all_passed = True
for i, source_markdown in enumerate(test_cases):
    try:
        tokens = tokenizer.transform(source_markdown)
        regenerated = markdown_transformer.transform(tokens)
        
        print(f'Test {i+1}: {\"✅ PASS\" if regenerated == source_markdown else \"❌ FAIL\"}')  
        if regenerated != source_markdown:
            print(f'  Expected: {repr(source_markdown)}')
            print(f'  Actual:   {repr(regenerated)}')
            all_passed = False
    except Exception as e:
        print(f'Test {i+1}: ❌ ERROR - {e}')
        all_passed = False
